Adversarial Estimation of Binary Configuration Probabilities under Sparse Dependence


Abstract
We consider the problem of estimating probabilities of binary configurations, which is common in operations management applications, including product bundling, advertising, etc. Existing approaches typically model each binary component as a category and apply multinomial models to estimate the choice probabilities. While computationally convenient, these methods do not exploit independence structures in the joint distribution and may therefore be statistically inefficient when the total number of items is large. Using a representation from Bahadur (1959) , we relate the sparsity of the generalized correlation coefficients to the (in)dependence structure of the binary components. We formulate the problem as estimating a high-dimensional vector of generalized correlation coefficients, together with low or moderate-dimensional nuisance parameters corresponding to the marginal probabilities. We develop a regularized adversarial estimator that attains the optimal rate under standard regularity conditions while remaining computationally feasible. The framework naturally extends to settings with covariates. We apply the proposed estimators to estimating average treatment effects with multiple binary treatments and show substantial finite-sample improvements over non-adaptive methods. Numerical studies corroborate the theoretical results.



\SUBJECTCLASS

Statistics: Localized nonparametric adversarial estimation;
Probability: Multivariate binary distributions.

keywords
Binary configuration
probability estimation; Localized adversarial approach; Sparse dependence.

1
Introduction

Many empirical applications require estimating the joint distribution of binary configurations, including purchase basket
(
Chung and Rao 2003
,
Gabel and Timoshenko 2022
, e.g.)
, advertising exposures
(
Aouad and Segev 2021
,
Li et al. 2025
, e.g.)
, clinical outcomes

(
Gueorguieva and
Agresti 2001
,
Agresti and Hartzel 2000
, e.g.)
, and multiple treatment assignments
(
Imbens and Rubin 2015
,
Arkhangelsky et al. 2024
,
Fitzmaurice et al. 2008
, e.g.)
. In multi-purchase applications, each configuration indicates whether a product is selected, so the joint distribution determines the probability of every possible bundle and captures cross-product purchase dependence. In causal inference settings, the configurations indicate assignment of multiple binary treatments, and their conditional joint distribution yields the generalized propensity score for each treatment combination. Despite their different interpretations, both settings share the same statistical primitive, a
(conditional) joint distribution over a binary configuration.

In many applications, the joint distribution exhibits (partial) independence across products or treatments. For example, in retail demand models, purchase decisions are sometimes approximately independent across product categories
(
Donnelly et al. 2021
, e.g.)
. In marketing, only a limited set of interaction effects is typically modeled across media channels or competing ads, implying independence across groups of exposures
(
Zantedeschi et al. 2017
,
Danaher et al. 2008
, e.g.)
. In clinical trials, certain combinations of drugs or therapies are designed or assumed to act independently
(
Pomeroy et al. 2022
,
Plana et al. 2022
, e.g.)
. To estimate probabilities of the binary configurations for
M
items, most existing methods do not exploit independence structures among products or treatments and can be inefficient when
M
is large. Methods that exploit such structure typically rely on strong modeling assumptions, such as sparse multivariate logit models
(
Hruschka 2026
, e.g.)
, factorization models
(
Gopalan et al. 2014
, e.g.)
, low-dimensional latent feature representation
(
Ruiz et al. 2020
, e.g.)
, shared confounders
(
Ranganath and Perotte 2018
, e.g.)
or independence of pre-specified categories
(
Donnelly et al. 2021
, e.g.)
.

In contrast, our approach exploits sparsity in the dependence structure to construct estimators with improved finite-sample performance and stronger theoretical guarantees. We only assume that the underlying dependence structure is sparse, without requiring knowledge of its support. Without additional structural assumptions, the distribution of an
M
-dimensional binary vector involves
2^{M}-1
parameters
(
Bahadur 1959
)
, consisting of
M
marginal probabilities and
2^{M}-M-1

generalized correlation coefficients
. Independence among some components could reduce the parameter number substantially. So in our setup, sparsity naturally arises from the intrinsic sparse generalized correlation structure across binary components. In Section
2.1
, we provide examples that connect our formulation to classical bundle and multipurchase choice models, including bundle-level multinomial logit
(
Jasin et al. 2024
,
Bai et al. 2024
,
Tulabandhula et al. 2023
,
Abdallah et al. 2026
, e.g.)
and Markov choice models
(
El Housni et al. 2025
,
Blanchet et al. 2016
,
Feldman and Topaloglu 2017
,
Şimşek and
Topaloglu 2018
,
Désir et al. 2020
, e.g.)
. Conditional on a fixed assortment, our framework represents the induced joint distribution of purchase incidences through marginal purchase probabilities and generalized correlation coefficients, without imposing a specific utility-shock distribution or transition process.

To estimate the marginal probabilities and generalized correlation coefficients in this representation, a natural starting point is the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE). However, the log-likelihood function is highly non-concave in the marginal probabilities, making optimization computationally infeasible, and the MLE tends to overfit when
2^{M}
is comparable to or exceeds
N
. Moreover, the MLE fails to adapt to the underlying sparse generalized correlation structure among the binary components. Notably, this non-concavity concerns only the
M
marginal probabilities, for which consistent estimators are readily available (e.g., sample averages). Conditional on these marginal probabilities, the remaining
2^{M}-M-1
generalized correlation coefficients yield a concave log-likelihood. This naturally motivates a two-stage plug-in approach: first estimate the marginal probabilities, then plug them in to estimate the generalized correlation coefficients via a convex program with an
\ell_{1}
penalty to exploit sparsity
(
Tibshirani 1996
,
Tibshirani 1997
,
Zou 2006
,
Belloni et al. 2014
, e.g.)
. We treat the
M
marginal probabilities as nuisance parameters and the
2^{M}-M-1
generalized correlation coefficients as the parameters of interest. This formulation leads to a high-dimensional estimand in the presence of low- to moderate-dimensional nuisance parameters. In contrast, the debiased machine learning literature
(
Chernozhukov et al. 2017a
, e.g.,)
typically considers low-dimensional target parameters with high-dimensional nuisance components. We show that a plug-in approach induces misspecification in the design matrix and fails to achieve rate optimality. While from a statistical performance we want to adapt to the intrinsic dimension, from a computational perspective, the dimension of the problem is
p\asymp 2^{M}
.

To address these challenges, we propose two regularized adversarial estimators. The first is an
\ell_{1}
-regularized estimator using a modified log-likelihood, namely the minimum log-likelihood over a set of marginal probabilities that are consistent with the data. This conservative approach allows for a lower regularization bias which makes its rate of convergence faster than the plug-in approach. Particularly, the term “adversarial” follows established usage in statistics and econometrics
(
Chernozhukov et al. 2026
,
Kaji et al. 2023
, e.g.,)
, which characterizes the max-min structure of our estimator, in which the marginal probability is chosen “adversarially” from the data-consistent set. However, this estimator is computationally intractable due to the inner minimization over the marginals. We therefore introduce a novel first-order estimator, which approximates the log-likelihood via a first-order expansion in the marginal probabilities, yielding an objective that is concave in the marginals. We construct the adversarial set for the marginal probabilities as a hyper-rectangle, so that the inner minimization is attained at one of its vertices. Since the objective remains concave in the generalized correlation coefficients, both the inner minimization and outer maximization are computationally tractable. This estimator matches the performance of the adversarial estimator and is rate-optimal under mild conditions, achieving the same rate as in the oracle case where the marginal probabilities are known.

Contributions

First, we propose a novel method for estimating the joint probabilities of binary configurations that adapts to the independence structure among
M
items using the Bahadur representation
(
Bahadur 1959
)
without explicit structural assumptions. Second, we develop an adversarial estimation approach for high-dimensional estimand in the presence of low-dimensional nuisance parameters. When the support of the generalized correlation coefficients contains at most
s
nonzero terms, without assuming their locations, we show that the oracle estimator achieves a rate of
\sqrt{sM/N}
(the standard Lasso rate) when the marginal probabilities are known. When the marginals are unknown, the plug-in estimator attains a rate of
M\sqrt{s/N}
, while the first-order estimator achieves
\sqrt{sM/N}+\sqrt{M\log M/N}
. Consequently, the first-order estimator is rate-optimal when
s\gtrsim\log M
. Third, we extend the framework to settings with low-dimensional covariates, which require estimating multiple nuisance functions. We establish a similar convergence rate for the
localized first-order estimator
, which outperforms the
localized plug-in estimator
, as supported by our simulation studies. Finally, we apply our framework to causal inference with many binary treatments, where estimating generalized propensity scores
(
Rosenbaum and Rubin 1983
,
Imbens 2000
, e.g.,)
is challenging. Exploiting independence structures, our estimators are consistent and efficient, with substantial finite-sample gains when the number of treatment combinations is large. Simulations show that our approach outperforms the multinomial logit model
(
Imai and Van Dyk 2004
,
Li and Li 2019
,
Yang et al. 2016
, e.g.)
commonly used for multi-level treatments.

Related Literature.

Some existing works also study the estimation of joint probabilities of binary configurations based on
Bahadur (1959)
. For example,
Sinha et al. (2010)
fits a Bahadur representation with higher-order terms set to zero, while
Yuan and Qu (2021)
approximates the joint distribution of within-community connectivities using a truncated Bahadur representation. Unlike these works, we consider the full Bahadur representation without assuming that higher-order terms vanish or that the support of the nonzero terms is known; instead, we impose only a sparsity assumption. The additional structure of binary configurations has also motivated alternative modeling approaches
(
Raginsky et al. 2013
,
Banerjee et al. 2008
,
Urschel et al. 2017
,
Rachkovskij and
Slipchenko 2012
,
Rachkovskij 2001
,
Rachkovskij et al. 2013
,
Cox 1972
,
Rao Chaganty and Joe 2004
,
Gyllenberg et al. 1997
,
Freund and Haussler 1991
,
Ichimura and Thompson 1998
, e.g.)
. Specifically, our formulation complements the literature on the conditional independence for undirected graphical models
(
Loh and Wainwright 2012
,
Morrison et al. 2017
,
Van Borkulo et al. 2014
,
Fan et al. 2017
)
, including classical Ising model
(
Cipra 1987
,
Newell and Montroll 1953
)
. The Ising model, restricted to pairwise dependencies, may still exhibit nonzero higher-order correlations, precluding sparsity in our representation. As discussed in Remark
3.1
, conditional independence in binary graphical models and sparsity of the Bahadur generalized correlation coefficients
(
Bahadur 1959
)
are distinct; Examples
3.2
and
3.3
further show that neither implies the other. Our localized estimator relates to a rich literature on the local maximum likelihood estimation
(
Fan et al. 1998
,
Newey 1994
,
Stone 1977
,
Tibshirani and Hastie 1987
,
Lewbel 2007
,
Hansen 2008
)
. Our method is also related to recent developments on adversarial approach, including regularized M-estimation
(
Hirshberg and Wager 2021
)
, adversarial Riesz representation
(
Chernozhukov et al. 2026
)
, and distributionally robust optimization
(
Blanchet and Murthy 2019
,
Blanchet et al. 2019
)
.

2
Problem Formulation

We have
N
i.i.d. observations
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{N}
where
X_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
for some compact set
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
d\geq 1
and
Y_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{M}
. For example, in the bundle-purchase setting,
X_{i}
collects contextual information relevant to customer
i
’s purchase decision, such as product attributes and customer demographics, while
Y_{i}
is the corresponding incidence vector indicating which products from the offered assortment
[M]
are selected. A customer may select any subset of the assortment, including no products, with no restriction on bundle size, and this setting is also considered by
(
Ruiz et al. 2020
,
Donnelly et al. 2021
, e.g.)
. We begin with characterizing
\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}=y\mid X_{i}=x)
by extending the unconditional representation of
\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}=y)
by
Bahadur (1959)
. For a binary vector
y\in\{0,1\}^{M}
and marginal probabilities
\alpha=(\alpha_{01},\ldots,\alpha_{0M})^{\prime}\in(0,1)^{M}
, we define the
j
-th standardized component as

\displaystyle z_{j}(y,\alpha):=(y_{j}-\alpha_{0j})/\sqrt{\alpha_{0j}(1-\alpha_{0j})}.

(1)

For
j\in[M]:=\{1,2,\ldots,M\}
, we denote the
j
-th conditional marginal probability as
\alpha_{0j}(x)=\mathbb{P}(Y_{ij}=1\mid X_{i}=x)
, which is the
j
-th component of vector
\alpha_{0}(x)\in(0,1)^{M}
. Note
\mathbb{E}[z_{j}(Y_{i},\alpha_{0}(x))\mid X_{i}=x]=0
and
\mathrm{Var}[z_{j}(Y_{i},\alpha_{0}(x))\mid X_{i}=x]=1
. A
k
-tuple
\ell=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{k}\}\subseteq[M]
such that
k=|\ell|\geq 2
represents an interaction subset. Since there are in total of
p=2^{M}-M-1
such possible subsets, we index each component
l\in[p]
by its corresponding configuration
\ell=\ell(l)
. Given covariate
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the
generalized correlation coefficients
are
r_{0}(x):=\left(r_{01}(x),\ldots,r_{0p}(x)\right)
, where for any
l\in[p]
,

r_{0l}(x):=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{j\in\ell(l)}z_{j}(Y_{i},\alpha_{0}(X_{i}))\bigg|X_{i}=x\right].

(2)

So each entry
r_{0l}(x)
corresponds to an
\ell(l)
such that
|\ell(l)|\geq 2
.
Bahadur (1959)
characterizes the joint distribution of a multivariate binary vector in the absence of covariates, and its extension to the conditional distribution of all binary components given covariates is straightforward, following Propositions 1 and 2 in
Bahadur (1959)
:

Theorem 2.1

\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}=y\mid X_{i}=x)=f(y,\alpha_{0}(x),r_{0}(x))\prod_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{0j}(x)^{y_{j}}(1-\alpha_{0j}(x))^{1-y_{j}}
for any
y\in\{0,1\}^{M}
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
, where
\displaystyle f(y,\alpha_{0}(x),r_{0}(x)):=1+\sum_{\ell(l)\subseteq[M]:\left|\ell(l)\right|\geq 2}r_{0l}(x)\prod_{j\in\ell(l)}z_{j}(y,\alpha_{0}(x))
.

Theorem
2.1
characterizes the dependence between components through the generalized correlation coefficients
r_{0}(x)=(r_{0l}(x))_{l\in[p]}
within function
f
. By defining a
p
-dimensional vector
W(\alpha,y)
where for any
l\in[p]
, its
l
-th component is equal to
W_{l}(\alpha,y):=\prod_{j\in\ell(l)}z_{j}(y,\alpha)
, we can write the function
f
as an affine function of the generalized correlation coefficients:
f(y,\alpha_{0}(x),r_{0}(x))=1+W(\alpha_{0}(x),y)^{\prime}r_{0}(x)
.
If all the binary components are independent,
r_{0}(x)=0
and
f(y,\alpha_{0}(x),0)=1
, which recovers the conditional likelihood of
y
given
x
as
\prod_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{0j}(x)^{y_{j}}(1-\alpha_{0j}(x))^{1-y_{j}}
under full independence. According to (
2
), for any
l\in[p]
, if within
\ell(l)
there is at least one binary component independent from the others conditioning on the covariates, then
r_{0l}(x)=0
. This connects independence structure with zero value of a generalized correlation coefficient. Thus in applications of interest where independence holds among some of the binary components, we can expect the vector
r_{0}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
to be sparse. Theorem
2.1
implies that estimating the binary configuration probabilities requires estimating
\alpha_{0}(x)
and
r_{0}(x)
. Before presenting our theoretical results, we first relate our probability representation to established models of multipurchase choice. We then begin the theoretical analysis with the
no-covariate case
, where the target is the unconditional probability
\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}=y)
. Building on these results, we extend the proposed estimators to the
with-covariate case
, where the target is the conditional probability
\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}=y\mid X_{i}=x)
.

2.1
Connection to Multipurchase Choice Models

Multipurchase models typically induce purchase probabilities over utility-maximizing bundles. Conditional on a fixed assortment
[M]
, our framework directly represents the joint binary distribution through marginal purchase probabilities
\alpha_{0}
and generalized correlation coefficients
r_{0}
, where
\alpha_{0j}=\mathbb{P}(Y_{j}=1)
is the marginal purchase probability of product
j
, while the components of
r_{0}
are standardized dependence coefficients of the joint bundle distribution. This avoids imposing a particular utility-shock distribution or transition process and permits the estimator to exploit unknown sparsity in the joint purchase probabilities.
The following examples illustrate how structural interpretations of these parameters arise under two particular bundle-level choice models: bundle-level MNL model and Markov choice model.

Example 2.2

(Bundle-level MNL Model)

Let
[M]
denote the offered assortment. For simplicity, we focus on the setting without covariates. Following Theorem
2.1
, the probability of purchasing the bundle characterized by
y\in\{0,1\}^{M}
is
q(y)=f(y;\alpha_{0},r_{0})\prod_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{0j}^{y_{j}}(1-\alpha_{0j})^{1-y_{j}}
. Assume first that
q(y)>0
for every
y
. Define the utility of bundle
y
as
V_{\alpha,r}(y)=\sum_{j=1}^{M}y_{j}\log\frac{\alpha_{0j}}{1-\alpha_{0j}}+\log f(y;\alpha_{0},r_{0})
. If the bundles are treated as alternatives and their utility shocks are i.i.d. Gumbel, then the saturated bundle-level MNL representation yields
q(y)=\frac{\exp(V_{\alpha,r}(y))}{\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{M}}\exp(V_{\alpha,r}(z))}
. If
q(y)=0
, the representation requires the extended utility
V_{\alpha_{0},r_{0}}(y)=-\infty
(equivalently, exclusion of that
bundle). We next illustrate how generalized correlation coefficients characterize local substitution patterns within a random-utility framework. Suppose a consumer chooses a bundle
Y\in\{0,1\}^{M}
and the deterministic utility of bundle
y
at price
p\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{M}
is
V(y;p)=V_{0}(y)-\beta p^{\top}y
where
\beta>0
. Recall that
\alpha_{0k}=\mathbb{P}(Y_{k}=1)
. Under the bundle-logit model (with i.i.d. Gumbel utility shocks), for any distinct
j,k\in[M]
,
\frac{\partial\alpha_{0k}}{\partial p_{j}}=-\beta\mathrm{Cov}(Y_{j},Y_{k})=-\beta r_{0,jk}\sqrt{\alpha_{0j}(1-\alpha_{0j})\alpha_{0k}(1-\alpha_{0k})}
. Hence, the cross-price elasticity is
\varepsilon_{kj}(p):=\frac{\partial\log\alpha_{0k}}{\partial\log p_{j}}=-\beta p_{j}r_{0,jk}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{0j}(1-\alpha_{0j})(1-\alpha_{0k})}{\alpha_{0k}}}
. Thus
r_{0,jk}<0
implies products
j,k
are local price substitutes;
r_{0,jk}>0
implies a negative cross-price effect and hence local price complementarity;
r_{0,jk}=0
implies no first-order cross-price effect. The own-price elasticity is
\varepsilon_{jj}(p)=-\beta p_{j}(1-\alpha_{0j})<0
.

Furthermore, the structural bundle-MNL specification satisfies independence of
irrelevant alternatives (IIA) at the bundle level. Let

S^{\prime}\subseteq S\subseteq[M]
, with feasible bundle sets

\mathcal{Y}(S^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{Y}(S)
, and define
q(y;S)=\frac{\exp\{V(y;p)\}}{\sum_{w\in\mathcal{Y}(S)}\exp\{V(w;p)\}}
.
Suppose that the utility of each retained bundle remains unchanged when the
assortment contracts from
S
to
S^{\prime}
. Then
\frac{q(y;S)}{q(z;S)}=\exp\{V(y;p)-V(z;p)\}=\frac{q(y;S^{\prime})}{q(z;S^{\prime})}
for any

y,z\in\mathcal{Y}(S^{\prime})
.
Thus, assortment contraction does not affect the relative odds of any two
retained bundles. This bundle-level IIA property does not, however, imply an
analogous property for individual-product marginal purchase probabilities,
because products are components of overlapping bundle alternatives rather than
mutually exclusive alternatives. Under the same utility-invariance condition, removing product
j
from the
full assortment yields the distributions conditional on

Y_{j}=0
. Consequently, for any
k\neq j
,

\mathbb{P}(Y_{k}=1\mid Y_{j}=0)-\mathbb{P}(Y_{k}=1)=-\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(Y_{j},Y_{k})}{1-\alpha_{0j}}=-\frac{r_{0,jk}\sqrt{\alpha_{0j}(1-\alpha_{0j})\alpha_{0k}(1-\alpha_{0k})}}{1-\alpha_{0j}}
, where the marginal probabilities and correlations are evaluated under the
full assortment. Hence,
r_{0,jk}<0
implies that removing product
j
increases
demand for product
k
, whereas
r_{0,jk}>0
implies that it decreases demand
for product
k
. As a special case, suppose that each customer purchases at most one product. Then, for every
k\neq j
,
Y_{j}Y_{k}=0,\operatorname{Cov}(Y_{j},Y_{k})=-\alpha_{0j}\alpha_{0k}
,
which implies
\mathbb{P}(Y_{k}=1\mid Y_{j}=0)=\frac{\alpha_{0k}}{1-\alpha_{0j}}
.
Thus, the probability mass associated with product
j
is redistributed among
the remaining products in proportion to their original choice probabilities.
Moreover, under
V(y;p)=V_{0}(y)-\beta p^{\top}y
,
the cross-price elasticity satisfies
\varepsilon_{kj}:=\frac{\partial\alpha_{0k}}{\partial p_{j}}\frac{p_{j}}{\alpha_{0k}}=-\beta p_{j}\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(Y_{j},Y_{k})}{\alpha_{0k}}=\beta p_{j}\alpha_{0j},\ k\neq j
, recovering the
proportional-substitution property of the classical product-level MNL model.

\blacksquare

Example 2.3

(Markov Chain Choice Model)

Suppose that
\mathbb{P}(Y=y)=\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}=y_{1})\prod_{j=2}^{M}\mathbb{P}(Y_{j}=y_{j}|Y_{j-1}=y_{j-1})
and define
q_{j,01}=\mathbb{P}(Y_{j}=1|Y_{j-1}=0)
,
q_{j,11}=\mathbb{P}(Y_{j}=1|Y_{j-1}=1)
. Let
\sigma_{j}^{2}=\alpha_{0j}(1-\alpha_{0j})
be the variance of
Y_{j}
, then
q_{j,01}=\alpha_{0j}-r_{0,\{j-1,j\}}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{0,j-1}}{1-\alpha_{0,j-1}}}
,
q_{j,11}=\alpha_{0j}+r_{0,\{j-1,j\}}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{\frac{1-\alpha_{0,j-1}}{\alpha_{0,j-1}}}
. So the Markov chain can be equivalently parametrized by
(\alpha_{01},\ldots,\alpha_{0M},r_{0,12},r_{0,23},\ldots,r_{0,\{M-1,M\}})
. Particularly,
r_{0,ij}=\prod_{m=i+1}^{j}r_{0,\{m-1,m\}}
. Given products
i,j\in[M]
,
\mathbb{P}(Y_{j}=1|Y_{i}=1)-\mathbb{P}(Y_{j}=1|Y_{i}=0)=r_{0,ij}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\sigma_{i}}
. So
r_{0,ij}
can be viewed as a standardized end-to-end demand-propagation coefficient in the Markov choice model defined. Particularly, suppose a promotion increases
\alpha_{0i}
while leaving downstream transition kernels unchanged. Then
\frac{d\alpha_{0j}}{d\alpha_{0i}}=r_{0,ij}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\sigma_{i}}
. If
m_{j}
is product
j
’s contribution margin, then the system-wide marginal value of increasing purchases of product
i
is
m_{i}+\sum_{j>i}m_{j}r_{0,ij}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\sigma_{i}}
. A promotion on a low-margin product can raise total profit if it increases subsequent purchases of high-margin products. Conversely, a promotion that increases focal-product sales may reduce expected total profit by crowding out profitable downstream
demand.
\blacksquare

Notation.

Throughout the paper,
\|\cdot\|_{1}
,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2}
(or
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
),
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\infty}
, and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{0}
denote the
\ell_{1}
-,
\ell_{2}
-,
\ell_{\infty}
-, and
\ell_{0}
-norms, respectively. For a positive integer
n
,
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
For a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}:=\max_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}|A_{ij}|
,
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{k,k}:=(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|A_{ij}|^{k})^{1/k}
,
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{0}
denotes the number of nonzero entries of
A
,
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
denotes the operator norm of
A
.
\mathbf{1}\{\cdot\}
denotes the indicator.
\propto
denotes proportionality. For any vector or matrix
V
,
V^{\prime}
denotes the transpose of
V
. Given
\delta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
T\subseteq[p]
, define
\delta_{T}
as
\delta
restricted to
T
. For random variables
Z
and
W
,
Z\perp\!\!\!\perp W
denotes independence. For any finite-dimensional real vector
\nu
,
\mathrm{dim}(\nu)
denotes its dimension.
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
denotes the Gaussian distribution with mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
. For deterministic sequences
{a_{n}}
and
{b_{n}}
, we write
a_{n}=\mathrm{O}(b_{n})
if
|a_{n}|\leq C|b_{n}|
for some constant
C>0
and all sufficiently large
n
, and
a_{n}=\mathrm{o}(b_{n})
if
a_{n}/b_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. For stochastic sequences,
X_{n}=\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{p}}(a_{n})
means
X_{n}/a_{n}
is bounded in probability, and
X_{n}=\mathrm{o}_{\mathrm{p}}(a_{n})
means
X_{n}/a_{n}\xrightarrow{p}0
,
X_{n}=\Omega_{p}(a_{n})
means
X_{n}/a_{n}
is bounded away from zero with non-vanishing probability. We write
a\gtrsim b
(respectively,
a\lesssim b
) if, for some universal constant
C>0
and all sufficiently large
N
,
a\geq Cb
(respectively,
a\leq Cb
).

3
Technical Assumptions

We start with assumptions for the with-covariate case. The no-covariate case can be viewed as a special case where the covariate space
\mathcal{X}=\{x\}
. In this case,
\alpha_{0}(x)=\alpha_{0}
,
r_{0}(x)=r_{0}
, and
\nabla_{x}r_{0}(x)=0
, so that the sparsity condition reduces to
\|r_{0}\|_{0}\leq s
, corresponding to condition (i) of Assumption
3
. We focus on estimating
r_{0}(x)
and
\alpha_{0}(x)
at a given covariate
x
in the interior of
\mathcal{X}
.

{assumption}

[Sparsity and regularity conditions]
(i)
\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|r_{0}(x)\|_{0}+\|\nabla r_{0}(x)\|_{0}\leq s
. (ii) For some positive constant
\bar{L}
,
{\displaystyle\max_{x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\{0,1\}^{M}}}1+W(\alpha_{0}(x),y)^{\prime}r_{0}(x)\leq\bar{L}
(iii)
{\displaystyle\min_{i\in[N]}}1+W(\alpha_{0}(X_{i}),Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0}(X_{i})>\xi>0
with probability
1-\epsilon
. (iv)
\alpha_{0j}(x)
is bounded away from
0
and
1
(i.e.
\exists\ \eta\in(0,1)
, such that
\eta<\alpha_{0j}(x)<1-\eta
) for all
j\in[M]
,
\forall x\in\mathcal{X}
. (v)
X
has density
\rho
such that
\rho(\cdot)\geq\rho_{0}>0
on
\mathcal{X}
and
\rho
is continuously differentiable in the interior of
\mathcal{X}
.

Condition (i) of Assumption
3
bounds the degree of sparsity on
r_{0}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\nabla r_{0}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d}
. When all binary components are independent from each other (for all values of
x\in\mathcal{X}
), we have
s=0
. The analysis also directly extends to a local measure of sparsity (i.e.,
s_{x}
). For instance, when only pairwise interactions are present and higher-order interactions are absent, we have
\|r_{0}(x)\|_{0}+\|\nabla r_{0}(x)\|_{0}\leq(d+1)\binom{M}{2}\ll 2^{M}
.
Conditions (ii) and (iii) impose upper and lower bounds on the deviations from full independence. For example, this condition holds in empirical settings where there exist
q_{1},q_{2}>0
such that the conditional probabilities are bounded within multiplicative factors
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
of the independent probabilities, i.e.,
q_{1}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{0j}(x)^{y_{j}}(1-\alpha_{0j}(x))^{1-y_{j}}\leq\mathbb{P}(Y_{i}=y\mid X_{i}=x)\leq q_{2}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{0j}(x)^{y_{j}}(1-\alpha_{0j}(x))^{1-y_{j}}
. Assumption
3
allows for situations where some combinations of treatments have zero probability (so no
Y_{i}
equal to that value exists in the data).

From a modeling perspective, sparsity of
r_{0}(x)
can be interpreted as localized dependence conditional on observed characteristics. For example, for customers shopping groceries, pasta, sauce, and cheese may be commonly purchased together, while the purchase of drinks are conditionally independent. Arbitrary dependence within this group can generate three nonzero pairwise coefficients and one three-way coefficient, whereas all coefficients involving other products vanish. Similarly, doctors have the option to provide (simultaneously) multiple treatments to a patient. Although several treatments may share an eligibility rule, others are assigned independently conditional on the patient records. Sparsity then allows rich dependence within a few operationally connected groups while ruling out most cross-group dependence.

Remark 3.1

The sparsity assumption in Assumption
3
differs from that in sparse Ising or binary graphical models, which impose sparsity on canonical interaction parameters. The two notions are fundamentally distinct: Even in a tree-structured Ising model, most pairwise correlations are typically nonzero, and Bahadur generalized correlation coefficients can be dense despite graph sparsity. Conversely, sparsity in the Bahadur generalized correlation coefficients does not imply a sparse conditional independence graph either. In particular, a distribution with only a single nonzero generalized correlation coefficient can still induce dense graphical conditional dependencies. Examples
3.2
and
3.3
below illustrate our argument. In both examples,
Y_{i}=(Y_{i1},Y_{i2},Y_{i3},Y_{i4})\in\{0,1\}^{4}
denotes the purchase decisions of the
i
-th customer for an assortment of four products. Suppose
\alpha_{0j}=\frac{1}{2}
for each
j\in[4]
, and
Z_{ij}:=z_{j}(Y_{ij},\alpha_{0j})=2Y_{ij}-1\in\{-1,+1\}
. We use
r_{0,\ell}
to denote the generalized correlation coefficient associated with the coordinate corresponding to bundle
\ell\subseteq[4]
.

\blacksquare

Example 3.2

Suppose
\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}))\propto\exp\{\theta(z_{1}z_{2}+z_{2}z_{3}+z_{3}z_{4})\}
with
\theta\neq 0
. This corresponds to a pairwise markov random field of the four-node Ising chain: “
1-2-3-4
” with only three edges
(1,2),(2,3),(3,4)
, and each node depends only on its neighbors, so
Z_{i1}\perp\!\!\!\perp(Z_{i3},Z_{i4})|Z_{i2}
,
Z_{i4}\perp\!\!\!\perp(Z_{i1},Z_{i2})|Z_{i3}
, etc.
This is a sparse conditional independence graph. However, all the pairwise generalized correlation coefficients are nonzero, since for some
\rho\neq 0
,
r_{0,12}=r_{0,23}=r_{0,34}=\rho
,
r_{0,13}=r_{0,24}=\rho^{2}
,
r_{0,14}=\rho^{3}
. Additionally,
r_{0,1234}=\rho^{2}
. So graphical model sparsity doesn’t imply the sparsity assumption in Assumption
3
.
\blacksquare

Example 3.3

Suppose
\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}))\propto\frac{(1+\gamma z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}z_{4})}{16}
where
0<|\gamma|<1
. Then
r_{0,jk}=0
for any pair
\{j,k\}
,
r_{0,jkl}=0
for any triple
\{j,k,l\}
, but
r_{0,1234}=\gamma\neq 0
. So
r_{0}
is sparse with the only nonzero element
r_{0,1234}
. Since
\mathbb{P}(Z_{i1}=z_{1},Z_{i2}=z_{2}|Z_{i3}=z_{3},Z_{i4}=z_{4})\propto 1+\gamma z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}z_{4}
, it can be checked directly that
\mathbb{P}(Z_{i1}=z_{1},Z_{i2}=z_{2}|Z_{i3}=z_{3},Z_{i4}=z_{4})\neq\mathbb{P}(Z_{i1}=z_{1}|Z_{i3}=z_{3},Z_{i4}=z_{4})\mathbb{P}(Z_{i2}=z_{2}|Z_{i3}=z_{3},Z_{i4}=z_{4})
unless
\gamma=0
. Thus
\mathbb{E}[Z_{i1}Z_{i2}|Z_{i3},Z_{i4}]\neq 0
. By symmetry,
\mathbb{E}[Z_{ik}Z_{ij}|Z_{il},Z_{im}]\neq 0
for all distinct
(k,j,l,m)
. So the binary graphical model has all the edges and is not sparse.
\blacksquare

The preceding examples illustrate that Bahadur and Ising sparsity encode different and generally non-nested forms of dependence. We focus on Bahadur sparsity because our objective is to estimate binary configuration probabilities directly: the Bahadur representation expresses these probabilities explicitly in terms of marginal probabilities and generalized dependence coefficients. By contrast, the Ising model relies on recovering conditional-dependence graphs. Although sparsity in its canonical parameters yields a sparse graph, computing marginal or configuration probabilities still requires the partition function, which is a normalizing term obtained by summing over all
2^{M}
configurations and depending on the full set of canonical parameters. Thus, Ising sparsity does not generally provide a comparably direct sparse representation of the probabilities that are our primary estimands.

Next, Assumption
3
imposes moment conditions on
W(\alpha_{0}(x),Y)
and
\nabla_{\alpha}W(\alpha_{0}(x),Y)^{\prime}r_{0}(x)
:

{assumption}
[Moment conditions]
Let
\widetilde{W}(\alpha_{0}(x),Y):=\frac{[\!W(\alpha_{0}(x),Y);\!\nabla_{\alpha}W(\alpha_{0}(x),Y)^{\prime}r_{0}(x)]}{1+W(\alpha_{0}(x),Y)^{\prime}r_{0}(x)}
. There exist absolute constants
\bar{\psi}_{2},\bar{K}_{m}>0
such that

{\displaystyle\min_{x\in\mathcal{X},l\in[p+M]}}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{W}_{l}^{2}(\alpha_{0}(x),Y)\mid X=x]^{1/2}\geq\bar{\psi}_{2}
,
{\displaystyle\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{l\in[p+M]}}\mathbb{E}[|\widetilde{W}_{l}(\alpha_{0}(X),Y)|^{m}\mid X=x]^{1/m}\leq\bar{K}_{m}
, where
m\in\{2,3\}
.

We further define
\bar{K}_{\max}:=\mathbb{E}[{\max_{l\in[p+M],i\in[N]}}\widetilde{W}_{l}^{2}(\alpha_{0}(X_{i}),Y_{i})]^{1/2}
, which may depend on
N
,
M
,
p
. A simple sufficient condition for Assumption
3
is that, uniformly over
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
l\in[p+M]
,
\widetilde{W}_{l}(\alpha_{0}(x),Y)
is conditionally sub-Gaussian with scale
\bar{K}
for some absolute constant
\bar{K}
, and has a conditional second moment bounded away from zero. This holds, for example, when
1+W(\alpha_{0}(x),Y)^{\prime}r_{0}(x)
is uniformly bounded above and away from zero and the numerator coordinates of
\widetilde{W}(\alpha_{0}(x),Y)
are uniformly conditionally sub-Gaussian and nondegenerate. In this case,
\bar{K}_{m}\lesssim\bar{K}\sqrt{m}
and
\bar{K}_{\max}\lesssim\bar{K}\sqrt{\log(N(p+M))}
, which becomes
\mathrm{O}\left(\bar{K}\sqrt{M+\log N}\right)
since
p\asymp 2^{M}
. The following assumption specifies the joint asymptotic regime governing
M
,
N
,
h
,
d
,
s
, where
h
denotes the bandwidth of the kernel estimator introduced later for the with-covariate case.

{assumption}

\sqrt{\frac{M^{3}}{Nh^{d}}}\leq\delta_{N}
,
sdh^{2}\leq\delta_{N}\xi
,
Nh^{d+4}\geq\frac{\log(NMd)}{d^{2}}
,
d^{2}h<1
,
M^{3}\leq N\delta_{N}^{2+d/2}\left(\frac{\xi}{sd}\right)^{d/2}
,
\delta_{N}\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{s}{d^{3}\xi},\frac{d^{d/(d+4)}M^{3/2}}{N^{\frac{2}{d+4}}[\log(NMd)]^{\frac{d}{2d+8}}}\right\}
,
\delta_{N}\rightarrow 0
,
h\rightarrow 0
,
Nh^{d}\rightarrow\infty
.

In the no-covariate case Assumption
3
implies
\sqrt{M^{3}/N}\leq\delta_{N}
. In the with-covariate case, by direct calculation, Assumption
3
implies that
h
satisfies
\left(M^{3}/(N\delta_{N}^{2})\right)^{1/d}\leq h\leq\sqrt{\delta_{N}\xi/(sd)}
. This interval is nonempty since we assume
M^{3}\leq N\delta_{N}^{2+\frac{d}{2}}\left(\xi/(sd)\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}
. Assumption
3
in the below imposes smoothness conditions:

{assumption}
[Smoothness conditions]
There exist deterministic sequences
L_{N}\geq 1
and

\rho_{\alpha,N}\rightarrow 0
such that as
N\rightarrow\infty
,
\sqrt{M}L_{N}\rho_{\alpha,N}=\mathrm{o}(\xi),L_{N}^{2}\rho_{\alpha,N}^{2}=\mathrm{o}(\xi)
, and with probability at least

1-\delta_{\alpha}
, the following conditions hold for the no-covariate case:

(1)
\alpha_{0}\in\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha}),\sup_{\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})}\|\tilde{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\|\leq\rho_{\alpha,N}
. For every
i\in[N]
,
a\mapsto W(a,Y_{i})
is twice
continuously differentiable on an open neighborhood of

\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
.

(2) Uniformly over
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
,
\|W(\alpha,Y_{i})\|_{\infty}+\max_{j\in[p],k\in[M]}|\partial_{\alpha_{k}}W_{j}(\alpha,Y_{i})|+\max_{j\in[p]}\|\nabla_{\alpha}^{2}W_{j}(\alpha,Y_{i})\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\nabla_{\alpha}\{W(\alpha,Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0}\}\|+\|\nabla_{\alpha}^{2}\{W(\alpha,Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0}\}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq L_{N}
.

Further, with probability at least
1-\delta_{\alpha}
, the following conditions hold for the with-covariate case:

(1)
\alpha_{*x}(X):=\alpha_{0}(x)+\nabla_{x}\alpha_{0}(x)(X-x)\in\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha})
,
\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha}),\|x^{\prime}-x\|\leq h\end{subarray}}\|\tilde{\alpha}(x^{\prime})-\alpha_{0}(x^{\prime})\|\leq\rho_{\alpha,N}
.

(2) Uniformly over all
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha})
, we have
\|W(\alpha(X_{i}),Y_{i})\|_{\infty}+\max_{j\in[p],k\in[M]}|\partial_{\alpha_{k}}W_{j}(\alpha(X_{i}),Y_{i})|+\max_{j\in[p]}\|\nabla_{\alpha}^{2}W_{j}(\alpha(X_{i}),Y_{i})\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\nabla_{\alpha}\{W(\alpha(X_{i}),Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0}(X_{i})\}\|+\|\nabla_{\alpha}^{2}\{W(\alpha(X_{i}),Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0}(X_{i})\}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq L_{N}
.

(3) The components of

\alpha_{0}(\cdot)
and
r_{0}(\cdot)
are twice continuously
differentiable in a neighborhood
U_{x}
of
x
containing
\{z:\|z-x\|_{2}\leq h\}
. There exists a deterministic constant
C_{x}<\infty
independent of
N,M,p,h
such that
\sup_{z\in U_{x}}\left\{\max_{k\in[M]}\|\nabla_{z}^{2}\alpha_{0k}(z)\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\max_{j\in[p]}\|\nabla_{z}^{2}r_{0j}(z)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\right\}\leq C_{x}
.

The adversarial sets
\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
,
\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha})
constructed in Section
5.3
make condition (1) for both no-covariate and with-covariate cases hold with high probability. Condition (3) for the with-covariate case is a general smoothness assumption on
r_{0}(\cdot)
and
\alpha_{0}(\cdot)
. For both the no-covariate and with-covariate settings, condition (2) provides a simple primitive condition for the more technical requirements used in the proofs. We defer these requirements, together with a detailed discussion, to Assumption
Appendix
in the Appendix. These conditions require sufficient smoothness in
\alpha_{0}(\cdot)
and
r_{0}(\cdot)
near the target covariate
x
for local-linear approximation. They also require
\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha})
to concentrate around
\alpha_{0}(\cdot)
, therefore to control the impact of estimation errors for the marginal probabilities on the Bahadur representation feature. In retail applications, this requires purchase dependence to vary smoothly with customer and product characteristics; in multi-level treatment applications, it analogously requires smooth changes in treatment-assignment dependence on the covariate information.

4
Proposed Estimators

We now turn to estimating
\alpha_{0}(\cdot)
and
r_{0}(\cdot)
. We first focus on the no-covariate case. We begin with the
maximum likelihood estimator
(MLE) as a natural baseline and discuss its limitations. Building on the MLE, we introduce a
plug-in estimator
that improves upon it both statistically and computationally. However, the plug-in estimator is not rate-optimal. To address this, we consider two
regularized adversarial estimators
. The first, termed the
adversarial estimator
, is a natural extension of the plug-in approach but is computationally infeasible. We therefore propose the
first-order estimator
as a second regularized adversarial estimator, which is computationally feasible and rate-optimal in common parameter regimes under mild conditions. Building on the results established for the no-covariate case, we then extend the proposed estimators to the with-covariate case, where we obtain localized counterparts of the plug-in, adversarial, and first-order estimators. Theoretical properties of these estimators parallel those in the no-covariate case, while the inclusion of covariates introduces additional technical challenges.

We first consider MLE for
(\alpha_{0},r_{0})
:

(\hat{\alpha}^{mle},\hat{r}^{mle})\in\argmax_{(\alpha,r)\in\mathbb{K}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\{\log(1+W(\alpha,Y_{i})^{\prime}r)+\sum_{j=1}^{M}Y_{ij}\log(\alpha_{j})+(1-Y_{ij})\log(1-\alpha_{j})\right\}

where
\mathbb{K}=\left\{(\alpha,r)\in(0,1)^{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}:1+W(\alpha,y)^{\prime}r\geq 0,\forall y\in\{0,1\}^{M}\right\}
includes
(\alpha,r)
leading to non-negative probability estimates for all possible configurations. However, the MLE faces practical limitations: First, the objective is non-concave in
\alpha
due to the standardized outcomes (
1
) in the formulation of vector
W(\alpha,Y_{i})
. Second, the number of parameters grows exponentially with
M
, leading to overfitting even for moderate values of
M
. Third, the MLE fails to exploit the underlying independence structure, which could otherwise improve estimation efficiency.

Although the objective function for MLE is non-concave in
\alpha
, fixing
\alpha
, the objective function of MLE becomes a concave maximization problem in
r
. Since independence implies sparsity in the coefficient vector
r_{0}
, the
\ell_{1}
-regularized estimator is particularly suitable. Moreover, consistent estimators for
\alpha_{0}
(e.g., sample averages) are readily available. A straightforward approach is therefore to first estimate
\alpha_{0}
by
\hat{\alpha}=(\hat{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\alpha}_{M})
, plug
\hat{\alpha}
into the objective of MLE, and apply an
\ell_{1}
penalty to
r
. The resulting estimator is referred to as the
plug-in estimator

\hat{r}^{\mathrm{PI}}
:

\hat{r}^{\mathrm{PI}}\!\in\!\arg\max_{r}\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log(1+W(\hat{\alpha},Y_{i})^{\prime}r)-\lambda\|r\|_{1,w},

(3)

where
\|r\|_{1,w}:=\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{j}|r_{j}|
is a weighted
\ell_{1}
-norm and
\alpha
are the nuisance parameters. Additionally, the weights are defined as
w_{j}:=\{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}W_{j}^{2}(\hat{\alpha},Y_{i})/(1+W(\hat{\alpha},Y_{i})^{\prime}\hat{r}^{(0)})^{2}\}^{1/2}
for any
j\in[p]
, where
\hat{r}^{(0)}
denotes an initial estimator for
r_{0}
. In particular, when
\alpha_{0}
is known, i.e.
\hat{\alpha}=\alpha_{0}
, we refer to the resulting estimator
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{oracle}}
as the oracle (plug-in) estimator. Theoretically, the ideal implementation would set
w_{j}=\{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}W_{j}^{2}(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})/(1+W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0})^{2}\}^{1/2}
, which balances the heterogeneity across components. However, since
\alpha_{0}
and
r_{0}
are unknown, we instead use their estimates
\hat{\alpha}
and
\hat{r}^{(0)}
. Although the weights depend on unknown quantities, stable estimates can be obtained via an iterative procedure with mild over-penalization. Note that
\mathrm{dim}(\alpha_{0})=M
and
\mathrm{dim}(r_{0})=2^{M}-M-1=p
, so
\mathrm{dim}(r_{0})\gg\mathrm{dim}(\alpha_{0})
as
M
grows. When
p\gg M
, we can obtain estimators for
\alpha_{0}
with a faster rate of convergence than for
r_{0}
. Specifically, for the rest of the paper,

\hat{\alpha}_{j}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}Y_{ij}\qquad\forall j\in[M]

(4)

are used in all no-covariate case estimators. Since
\alpha_{0j}\in(0,1)
, for sufficiently large
N
,
\hat{\alpha}_{j}(1-\hat{\alpha}_{j})>0
with high probability, so
z_{j}(y,\hat{\alpha}_{j})
is well defined. In practice if
\hat{\alpha}_{j}(1-\hat{\alpha}_{j})
becomes numerically close to zero, one can apply a mild truncation or add a small constant to the denominator to avoid division by zero.

Remark 4.1

In the estimation of
r_{0}
for the plug-in estimator, we do not impose the constraint on
\mathbb{K}
, because we are using
\hat{\alpha}
, and it is possible that
(\hat{\alpha},r_{0})\not\in\mathbb{K}
. By construction of the objective function (
3
), since
r=0
is always feasible, by optimality of
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{PI}}
we have
1+W(\hat{\alpha},Y_{i})^{\prime}\hat{r}^{\mathrm{PI}}>0
for every
Y_{i}
in the data, so the probability estimates is strictly positive for any outcome vector in the data. We also show in the proofs that under mild conditions,
1+W(\hat{\alpha},Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0}>0
for any
Y_{i}
in the data. Similar arguments hold for the estimators
\hat{r}^{A},\hat{r}^{\mathrm{FO}}
defined later. This result is sufficient in many applications, because practitioners typically only need probability estimates for binary configurations that are actually observed in the data. For example, in A/B testing or recommendation systems, a firm may only evaluate the probability of a limited set of products or advertisements presented to users, rather than all possible combinations of items. Similarly, in retail analytics, firms typically analyze purchase patterns among product bundles that have appeared in historical transactions, without requiring probability estimates for bundles that have never been offered. Related discussion on possible extensions is included in Section
8
.

\blacksquare

Although
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{PI}}
is computationally tractable, plugging in
\hat{\alpha}
induces mis-specification in the score function related to (
3
). The resulting discrepancy is dominated by the
\ell_{1}
estimation error
\|\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\|_{1}=\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{p}}(M/\sqrt{N})
, introducing additional sparsity-dependent factors and yielding a slower convergence rate than the typical Lasso rate. Detailed discussions are provided in Section
5
. Faced with the slower rate of convergence for the plug-in estimator, we propose an
adversarial estimator

\hat{r}^{A}
, defined as

\displaystyle\hat{r}^{A}\in\argmax_{r}\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log(1+W(\alpha,Y_{i})^{\prime}r)-\lambda\|r\|_{1,w}

(5)

where
\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
is an adversarial set such that
\alpha_{0}\in\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
with high probability. From a statistical perspective, the minimization of
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
acts as a regularization allowing us to better control the mis-specification due to unknown
\alpha_{0}
. This allows us to use a smaller penalty parameter
\lambda
, mitigating the bias induced by the overly large
\lambda
required by the plug-in estimator due to overfitting on
\hat{\alpha}
. Since the minimum of concave functions is still concave, the objective function (
5
) is concave in
r
. However, the inner problem of (
5
) requires the minimization over
\alpha
through the highly non-linear and non-concave mapping
W(\alpha,\cdot)
. This computational challenge motivates us to further propose the
first-order estimator

\hat{r}^{\mathrm{FO}}
, defined as

\hat{r}^{\mathrm{FO}}\in\argmax_{r}\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log\left(1+W^{\mathrm{FO}}(\alpha,Y_{i})^{\prime}r\right)-\lambda\|r\|_{1,w},

(6)

where
W^{\mathrm{FO}}(\alpha,Y_{i})=W(\hat{\alpha},Y_{i})+\nabla_{\alpha}W(\hat{\alpha},Y_{i})(\alpha-\hat{\alpha})
. Estimator (
6
) also adopts an adversarial approach but uses a first-order approximation of
W(\alpha,Y_{i})
with respect to
\alpha
around
\hat{\alpha}
. The estimator has a bilinear form and is concave in
\alpha
for any fixed
r
. Hence, the minimization of this concave function is attained at the extreme points of
\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
. For appropriately structured sets
\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
, such as a hyper-rectangle
\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})=\prod_{j=1}^{M}[\hat{\alpha}_{j}-c_{j},\hat{\alpha}_{j}+c_{j}]
, where
c_{j}
denotes the confidence interval half-width and
\hat{\alpha}_{j}
is defined by (
4
), the problem remains computationally feasible. As a result, the discrepancy in the score function becomes negligible since it includes only the second-order bias in the marginal probabilities, in contrast to the plug-in estimator
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{PI}}
. As shown in Theorem
5.3
, the
\lambda
for
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{FO}}
essentially bounds the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm of the score as if the marginal probabilities were known.

The insights and estimators from the no-covariate case extend naturally to the with-covariates case. However, the nuisance parameters associated with the marginal probabilities are now functions, which complicates the construction of the adversarial set, as discussed later. Given initial estimates
\hat{\alpha}(\cdot)=\left(\hat{\alpha}_{1}(\cdot),\ldots,\hat{\alpha}_{M}(\cdot)\right)
, the localized counterparts of the plug-in estimator (
3
) and the adversarial estimator (
5
) are defined in the Electronic Companion by (
27
) and (
28
). Our analysis below focuses primarily on the localized counterpart of the first-order estimator (
6
), which is defined as follows at any covariate value
x
:

\hat{r}^{\mathrm{FO}}(\cdot,x)\in\argmax_{a,b}\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha})}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}K_{h}\left(\mbox{$X_{i}-x$}\right)\log\left(1+W^{\mathrm{FO}}(\alpha(X_{i}),Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{a,b}(X_{i},x)\right)-\lambda_{x}\|r_{a,b}\|_{w_{x},h,1},

\mbox{where}\quad W^{\mathrm{FO}}(\alpha(X_{i}),Y_{i}):=W(\hat{\alpha}(X_{i}),Y_{i})+\nabla_{\alpha}W(\hat{\alpha}(X_{i}),Y_{i})(\alpha(X_{i})-\hat{\alpha}(X_{i})),

K_{h}(X_{i}-x):=\frac{1}{h^{d}}K\left(\frac{X_{i}-x}{h}\right)
,
K:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the kernel function, and
h
is the bandwidth. For
a\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
b\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d}
,
r_{a,b}(X_{i},x):=a+b(X_{i}-x)
,
\|r_{a,b}\|_{w_{x},h,1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{xj}|a_{j}|+h\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{d}w_{xjk}|b_{jk}|
, and the weights are
w_{xj}:=\left\{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}K_{h}(X_{i}-x)W_{j}(\hat{\alpha}(X_{i}),Y_{i})^{2}/(1+W(\hat{\alpha}(X_{i}),Y_{i})^{\prime}\hat{r}^{(0)}(X_{i},x))^{2}\right\}^{1/2}
,
w_{xjk}:=\left\{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}K_{h}(X_{i}-x)W_{j}(\hat{\alpha}(X_{i}),Y_{i})^{2}[(X_{ik}-x_{k})/h]^{2}/(1+W(\hat{\alpha}(X_{i}),Y_{i})^{\prime}\hat{r}^{(0)}(X_{i},x))^{2}\right\}^{1/2}
for any
j\in[p]
,
k\in[d]
, where
\hat{r}^{(0)}(X_{i},x)
denotes an initial estimator for
r_{0}(X_{i})
.

The estimates for
\alpha_{0j}(x)
can be obtained from different approaches. In this paper, we focus on the localized estimator (
10
) introduced in Section
5
, where we also describe the construction of the data-dependent adversarial set
\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha})
. All three objectives for the estimators in the covariate case are concave functions in
r_{a,b}
. At covariate
X_{i}
, the generalized correlation coefficient vector is
r_{0}(X_{i})
, where
r_{0}(X_{i})=r_{0}(x)+\nabla r_{0}(x)(X_{i}-x)+\Delta
and
\|\Delta\|_{\infty}=\mathrm{O}\left(\|X_{i}-x\|^{2}\right)
under general regularity conditions. Assumption
5.2
requires
K(\cdot)
to be supported on
\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|u\|\leq 1\}
. Then, by the definition of
K_{h}(\cdot)
, for any
X_{i}
such that
K_{h}(X_{i}-x)\neq 0
, we have
\mathrm{O}\left(\|X_{i}-x\|^{2}\right)=\mathrm{O}(h^{2})
. Hence,
r_{a,b}(X_{i},x)
effectively approximates
r_{0}(X_{i})
when
X_{i}
is close enough to
x
, where
a
approximates
r_{0}(x)
and
b
approximates its gradient
\nabla r_{0}(x)
. The minimization over
\alpha(\cdot)\in\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha})
is again attained at an extreme point of the nuisance function set
\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha})
.

However, care must be taken in specifying this set, as it can easily become too large. For instance, since the nuisance functions depend on
X_{i}
, one might be tempted to minimize over each
\alpha(X_{i})
separately, but this would create an overly adversarial setup that ignores the dependence across covariates
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
. We define the set of functions
\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha})
such that the function
\alpha_{*x}(X)=\alpha_{0}(x)+\nabla_{x}\alpha_{0}(x)(X-x)\in\mathcal{A}_{x}(\hat{\alpha})
with high probability. Additionally, the penalty for the gradient estimation term
b
is scaled by the bandwidth parameter
h
. Intuitively, since the estimation target is
r_{0}(x)
, the factor
h
attenuates the effect of the estimation error in
\nabla r_{0}(x)
through
b
. Note that
r_{a,b}(X_{i},x)
provides a first-order approximation to
r_{0}(X_{i})
, and higher-order refinements are possible in principle. Such extensions, however, would require additional regularity and smoothness assumptions. Below we include a remark on higher-order approximations:

Remark 4.2

One could alternatively define the localized estimator with higher order terms. For example,

r_{a,b,c}(X_{i},x):=a+b(X_{i}-x)+c[X_{i}-x,X_{i}-x]
, where
a\in\mathbb{R}^{p},b\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d},c\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d\times d}
,

\|r_{a,b,c}\|_{w_{x},h,1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{xj}|a_{j}|+h\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{d}w_{xjk}|b_{jk}|+h^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{m=1}^{d}w_{xjkm}|c_{jkm}|
, and
c[X_{i}-x,X_{i}-x]
is a contraction of the
p\times d\times d
tensor with two copies of
X_{i}-x
, producing a
p
-dimensional vector. This formulation increases the dimension of the parameter space, requires higher-order smoothness conditions and needs to account for the sparsity in the second-order derivative of
r_{0}(x)
. Meanwhile it reduces the approximation errors by improving the dependence on the bandwidth
h
.
\blacksquare

Our local-linear estimator is best suited for low-dimensional covariates because its effective sample size is
Nh^{d}
. For large
d
, if the conditional distribution depends on
X
through a low-dimensional representation
\psi(X)\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
where
q\ll d
, the estimator can be applied to
\psi(X)
, with
q
replacing
d
in the assumptions. Alternatively, under additional structural assumptions, the local-linear estimators could be replaced by regularized high-dimensional methods
(
Haris et al. 2022
,
Dong and Tu 2025
, e.g.)
. For example, each component of
\alpha_{0}(\cdot)
and
r_{0}(\cdot)
may be approximated using only a small subset of covariates
(
Lafferty and Wasserman 2008
,
Bertin and Lecué 2008
, e.g.)
, a sum of separate covariate effects
(
Ravikumar et al. 2009
,
Meier et al. 2009
, e.g.)
, or a function of a single linear combination of the covariates
(
Alquier and Biau 2013
,
Radchenko 2015
, e.g.)
.

We now define restricted eigenvalues that characterize the curvature over the restricted sets induced by the regularization, even when the design matrix is globally rank-deficient.
For a constant
c>1
, let
\bar{c}:=2\frac{c+1}{c-1}
. The restricted eigenvalues for both no-covariate and with-covariate cases are

\begin{array}[]{rl}\kappa_{\bar{c}}^{2}&\displaystyle:=\min_{\|v_{T^{c}}\|_{w,1}\leq\bar{c}\|v_{T}\|_{w,1},v\neq 0}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\{W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}v\}^{2}}{\{1+W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0}\}^{2}\|v_{T}\|_{w,2}^{2}},\\
\kappa_{x,\bar{c}}^{2}&\displaystyle:=\min_{\|v_{T_{x}^{c}}\|_{w_{x},h,1}\leq\bar{c}\|v_{T_{x}}\|_{w_{x},h,1},(v_{a},v_{b})\neq 0}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{K_{h}(X_{i}-x)\{W(\alpha_{0}(X_{i}),Y_{i})^{\prime}(v_{a}+v_{b}(X_{i}-x))\}^{2}}{\{1+W(\alpha_{0}(X_{i}),Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0}(X_{i})\}^{2}\|(v_{a},v_{b})_{T_{x}}\|_{w_{x},h,2}^{2}},\end{array}

(7)

where
T
is the support of
r_{0}
in the no-covariate case and
T_{x}
is the support of
(r_{0}(x),\nabla r_{0}(x))
in the covariate-case. In the definition for
\kappa_{\bar{c}}^{2}
,
v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
\|v_{T}\|_{w,k}=(\sum_{j\in T}w_{j}^{k}|v_{j}|^{k})^{1/k}
. For
v=(v_{a}^{\prime},v_{b}^{\prime})^{\prime}
in the definition for
\kappa_{x,\bar{c}}^{2}
,
v_{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
v_{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d}
.
\|(v_{a},v_{b})_{T_{x}}\|_{w_{x},h,k}=\big(\sum_{j\in T_{x}}w_{xj}^{k}|v_{aj}|^{k}+h^{k}\sum_{(j,l)\in T_{x}}w_{xjl}^{k}|v_{b,jl}|^{k}\big)^{1/k}
. The restricted eigenvalues are standard measures of curvature for analyzing Lasso-type estimators in high-dimensional statistics
(
Bickel et al. 2009
,
Bühlmann and Van
De Geer 2011
)
. However, new technical challenges arise in our analysis compared to the classical Lasso setting. For example, we need to account for the non-separability between marginal probabilities and the binary components, as manifested in the structure of the design matrix.

5
Theoretical Results

5.1
Rates of Convergence without Covariates

We now turn to the main theoretical results for the no-covariate case. We first compare the penalty parameters
\lambda
required by the oracle, plug-in, and first-order estimators, then present their convergence rates, and finally discuss the resulting statistical and computational trade-offs.

We begin by defining several quantities that determine the lower bounds on the penalty parameter
\lambda
for the estimators. When
\alpha_{0}
is known so that
\hat{\alpha}=\alpha_{0}
, define
\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}}
as follows to characterize the lower bound on the penalty parameter
\lambda
for the oracle estimator:

\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}}:=\frac{\Phi^{-1}(1-\delta/(2p))}{\sqrt{N}}\max_{j\in[p]}\frac{1}{w_{j}}\left\{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{W_{j}^{2}(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})}{(1+W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0})^{2}}\right\}^{1/2},

(8)

where
\Phi
denotes the cumulative distribution function of a standard normal random variable. The lower bounds
\underline{\lambda}^{\mathrm{PI}}
and
\underline{\lambda}^{\mathrm{FO}}
on the penalty parameters for the plug-in and first-order estimators can be directly related to the
\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}}
as follows

\underline{\lambda}^{\mathrm{PI}}:=\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}}+\frac{K_{\alpha,1}(1+\beta_{N})\sqrt{M}}{\xi}\|\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\|,\qquad\underline{\lambda}^{\mathrm{FO}}:=\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}}+\frac{K_{\alpha,2}(1+\beta_{N})}{\xi}\left\lVert\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\right\rVert^{2}.

(9)

where
\beta_{N}:=\max_{j\in[p]}\frac{1}{w_{j}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{|W_{j}(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})|}{1+W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0}}
and
K_{\alpha,1}
,
K_{\alpha,2}
as in Assumption
Appendix
.
Self-normalized moderate-deviation theory requires the penalty parameter
\lambda
to dominate the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm of the score
S(\alpha,r):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{W(\alpha,Y_{i})}{1+W(\alpha,Y_{i})^{\prime}r}
(similarly, denote
S^{\mathrm{FO}}(\alpha,r):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{W^{\mathrm{FO}}(\alpha,Y_{i})}{1+W^{\mathrm{FO}}(\alpha,Y_{i})^{\prime}r}
). For the oracle estimator we have
\|S(\alpha_{0},r_{0})\|_{\infty}=\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{P}}(\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}})
. For the plug-in and first-order estimators, the corresponding score norms satisfy with high probability,

\begin{array}[]{rl}\displaystyle\|S(\hat{\alpha},r_{0})\|_{\infty}\!\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}}+\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{P}}(\sqrt{M}\|\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\|),\ \ \ \displaystyle\|S^{\mathrm{FO}}(\alpha_{0},r_{0})\|_{\infty}\!\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}}+\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{P}}(\|\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\|^{2}),\end{array}

where
\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}}=\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{P}}(\sqrt{M/N})
. The additional term for the plug-in estimator arises from the estimation of
\alpha_{0}
and is
\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{P}}(\sqrt{M}\|\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\|)=\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{P}}(M/\sqrt{N})
, which dominates the oracle term. Consequently,
\underline{\lambda}^{\mathrm{PI}}=\mathrm{O}(M/\sqrt{N})
. By contrast, for the first-order estimator, the estimation error enters only through a second-order term, leaving
\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}}
as the dominant term so
\underline{\lambda}^{\mathrm{FO}}=\mathrm{O}(\sqrt{M/N})
.

Proposition
5.1
demonstrates the rate of convergence for the oracle estimator
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{oracle}}
.

Proposition 5.1

(Oracle estimator)

Suppose Assumptions
3
–
3
and the inequality in Assumption
3
(2) hold at
\alpha_{0}
. Additionally, suppose (i)
\lambda=c\lambda^{\mathrm{oracle}}
for some
c>1
, (ii) for some constant
C^{\prime}
,
\kappa_{\bar{c}}^{2}
is bounded away from zero and
\bar{K}_{2}+K_{\alpha,1}+K_{\alpha,2}/\xi^{2}\leq C^{\prime}
, where
K_{\alpha,1},K_{\alpha,2}
are defined in Assumption
Appendix
. Then for
N
sufficiently large and
C
independent of
s,N,M
, with probability
1-C\delta-C\delta_{\alpha}-C\epsilon
where
\frac{\log^{3}(p/\delta)}{N}=\mathrm{o}(1)
,

\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\{W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}(\hat{r}^{\mathrm{oracle}}-r_{0})\}^{2}}{(1+W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0})^{2}}}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{sM+s\log(1/\delta)}{N}}.

The oracle estimator has essentially the same rate of convergence as the typical Lasso rate for the high-dimensional linear model
\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{P}}(\sqrt{s\log p/N})
, as
M\asymp\log p
. Since the oracle estimator treats
\alpha_{0}
as known, condition (1) of Assumption
3
is unnecessary, and the remaining smoothness requirements need only hold at
\alpha=\alpha_{0}
. Specifically, for the lower-level conditions underlying Assumption
3
, it suffices that conditions (3) and (6) of Assumption
Appendix
hold, while conditions (2) and (4) of Assumption
Appendix
need only hold at
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha_{0}
, rather than uniformly over the adversarial set.

Proposition 5.2

(Plug-in estimator)

Suppose Assumptions
3
–
3
and Assumption
3
(1)-(2) hold. Suppose (i) for constants
C_{2}>C_{1}>1
,
C_{1}\underline{\lambda}^{\mathrm{PI}}\leq\lambda/c\leq C_{2}\underline{\lambda}^{\mathrm{PI}}
with probability at least
1-\delta
where
\frac{\log^{3}(p/\delta)}{N}=\mathrm{o}(1)
; (ii)
sM^{1/2}K_{\alpha,1}\|\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\|/\{\xi\kappa_{\bar{c}}^{2}\min_{j\in[p]}w_{j}^{2}\}\leq\delta_{N}
; (iii)
\kappa_{\bar{c}}^{2}
is bounded away from zero and
\bar{K}_{2}+K_{\alpha,1}+K_{\alpha,2}/\xi^{2}\leq C^{\prime}
for some constant
C^{\prime}
, where in (ii)-(iii),
\delta_{N}
is defined in Assumption
3
and
K_{\alpha,1},K_{\alpha,2}
are defined in Assumption
Appendix
. Then for
N
sufficiently large and
C
independent of
s,M,N
, with probability
1-C\delta-C\delta_{\alpha}-C\epsilon
,

\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\{W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}(\hat{r}^{\mathrm{PI}}-r_{0})\}^{2}}{(1+W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0})^{2}}}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{s(M+\log(1/\delta))}{N}}+C\sqrt{\frac{sM^{2}}{N}}+C\sqrt{\frac{sM\log(1/\delta)}{N}}.

Proposition
5.2
establishes the rate of convergence for the plug-in estimator. The plug-in estimator requires more technical assumptions than the oracle estimator because it needs to learn the marginal probabilities. Example
EC.1.2
in Appendix
EC.1.1
of the Electronic Companion demonstrates the sharpness of the rate established for
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{PI}}
in Proposition
5.2
. Proposition
5.2
implies that the rate of convergence for
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{PI}}
is
\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{P}}(\sqrt{sM^{2}/N})=\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{P}}(\sqrt{s/N}\log p)
if
s\geq 1
and
\log(1/\delta)=\mathrm{O}(M)
, which is slower than that of
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{FO}}
as shown by Theorem
5.3
below.

Theorem 5.3

(First-order estimator)

Suppose Assumptions
3
-
3
hold. Suppose (i) for a constant
C_{1}>1
,
\lambda/c\geq C_{1}\underline{\lambda}^{\mathrm{FO}}
with probability at least
1-\delta
where
\frac{\log^{3}(p/\delta)}{N}=\mathrm{o}(1)
; (ii)
s\|\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0}\|^{2}K_{\alpha,2}\bar{K}_{3}/\{\xi\kappa_{\bar{c}}^{2}{\displaystyle\min_{j\in[p]}}w_{j}^{2}\}\leq\delta_{N}
where
\delta_{N}
is defined in Assumption
3
and
K_{\alpha,2}
is defined in Assumption
Appendix
. Then for
N
sufficiently large,
C
independent of
s,N,M
, with probability
1-C\delta-C\delta_{\alpha}-C\epsilon
,

\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\{W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}(\hat{r}^{\mathrm{FO}}-r_{0})\}^{2}}{(1+W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0})^{2}}}\leq C\lambda\sqrt{s}\frac{(1+1/c)}{\kappa_{\bar{c}}}+C\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1/2}(\hat{\alpha}),

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\hat{\alpha})\leq C\bar{K}_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(M/\delta)}{N}}{\displaystyle\max_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})}}\|\alpha-\alpha_{0}\|_{1}+C\frac{K_{\alpha,2}}{\xi^{2}}{\displaystyle\max_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})}}\|\alpha-\alpha_{0}\|^{2}
.

For the first-order estimator,
W^{\mathrm{FO}}(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})
is a first-order expansion of
W(\alpha,Y_{i})
around
\hat{\alpha}
, so it introduces only a second-order bias in the score. This bias is smaller than the bias induced by directly plugging
\hat{\alpha}
into the score. By incorporating adversarial selection of
\alpha
, the first-order estimator regularizes the dependence of the score on
\hat{\alpha}
and mitigates overfitting. As a result, a smaller penalty level
\lambda
suffices compared with the plug-in estimator, leading to a lower estimation error. Moreover, the formulation of
\hat{r}^{\mathrm{FO}}
circumvents the non-concavity of the maximum likelihood objective and other computational challenges inherent in adversarial estimation of the marginal probabilities.

The following corollary specializes Theorem
5.3
to a representative case, providing a precise convergence rate under the specific choice of the set
\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
defined in (
13
), which admits efficient computation. The details of the construction of
\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
are in Section
5.3
.

Corollary 5.4

Under the conditions of Theorem
5.3
, suppose
\lambda/c\leq C_{2}\underline{\lambda}^{\mathrm{FO}}
for some constant
C_{2}>C_{1}>1
,
\kappa_{\bar{c}}
is bounded away from zero and
\bar{K}_{2}+K_{\alpha,1}+K_{\alpha,2}/\xi^{2}\leq C^{\prime}
for some constant
C^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
is constructed as (
13
) and
0<\delta\leq\delta_{\alpha}
. Then for
N
sufficiently large and
C
independent of
s,N,M
, with probability
1-C\delta-C\delta_{\alpha}-C\epsilon
,

\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\{W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}(\hat{r}^{\mathrm{FO}}-r_{0})\}^{2}}{(1+W(\alpha_{0},Y_{i})^{\prime}r_{0})^{2}}}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{s(M+\log(1/\delta))}{N}}+C\sqrt{\frac{M\log(M/\delta)}{N}}.

Corollary
5.4
shows that the impact of estimated marginal probabilities on the first-order estimator is captured by
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}(\hat{\alpha})=\mathrm{O}_{\mathrm{p}}\left(\frac{M\log M}{N}\right)
defined as in Theorem
5.3
. In many regimes of interest (e.g.,
s\gg\log M
), this term is expected to be small as the adversarial estimation is over a nuisance parameter with dimension
M\ll p
. Note that the
\log M
factor arises from the
\ell_{\infty}
construction of
\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
in (
13
), which requires controlling the maximum deviation across the
M
marginal probability estimators. This factor could be avoided by instead defining
\mathcal{A}(\hat{\alpha})
using an
\ell_{2}
bound on the marginal estimation error (i.e., Euclidean balls). However, from a computational standpoint, representing the adversarial set as a hyper-rectangle is considerably more tractable, because the minimum of the inner optimization problem in (
6
) is attained at one of its finitely many extreme points. Since
M\asymp\log p
, Corollary
5.4
implies that the first-order estimator attains a convergence rate of order
\sqrt{sM/N}+\sqrt{M\log(M)/N}
. The rate for the oracle estimator with
\alpha_{0}
known is of order
\sqrt{sM/N}
. So the first-order estimator is rate optimal when
s\gtrsim\log(M)
. Table
1
compares all the aforementioned estimators when
s\gtrsim\log(M)
, showing the trade off between rates of convergence and computational complexity.

Table 1:
Comparison of estimators when
s\gtrsim\log(M)
.

\lambda

Rate

r

Evaluation over
\alpha

concave

non-concave maximization

\hat{\alpha}
)

\sqrt{\log p/N}

tractable minimization

\alpha_{0}
)
